# Texas oil and gas leases named for estates, heirs, trusts and ranches, by county

A finding aid for county genealogy societies, family historians and heirs. Many Texas oil and gas leases carry the family name of
the landowner who signed them, often with ESTATE, HEIRS, TRUST or RANCH in the name. This list gathers those leases from the
Railroad Commission of Texas's production reports, January 1993 through August 2026.

**15,675 leases** reported in 2020 or later (10,073 still producing): 1,052 with HEIRS,
3,027 with ESTATE, 1,996 with TRUST and 9,600 with RANCH in the name. Another
**10,006** reported only between 1993 and 2019. Across 207 counties.

How to read it:

- A lease name is what the operator filed with the Railroad Commission. It usually names the family or estate that owned the land
  when the lease was signed. It does **not** show who owns the minerals today; that is in the county's deed records and on the
  appraisal district's mineral roll.
- **First report** is the first month in these records, which begin in January 1993; a lease showing 1993 may be much older.
- Each county lists up to 12 leases that have a page (HEIRS, ESTATE and TRUST before RANCH, producing first, oldest first) and up
  to 6 that stopped before 2020. The county's lease list (second link under each county) covers every lease there with at least
  a year of reports since 2020.
- Counts by word use the first of HEIRS, ESTATE, TRUST or RANCH in the name, so each lease is counted once.
- Leases that stopped before 2020 have no page; look them up by district and lease number in the Railroad Commission's
  [Production Data Query](https://webapps.rrc.texas.gov/PDQ/home.do).
